    Headline
    anaphylactic shock, anaphylactoid reactions, angioedema, bronchospasm, pruritus, rash (unspecified), urticaria
    General Information
    Allergic reactions to buprenorphine; naloxone have occurred and are usually typified by pruritus, urticaria, or rash (unspecified). Although rare, cases of anaphylactic shock, bronchospasm, and anaphylactoid reactions, such as angioedema, have been reported. Use of this medication is contraindicated in patients with a history of hypersensitivity to buprenorphine or naloxone.

    Headline
    opioid-induced esophageal dysfunction
    General Information
    Cases of opioid-induced esophageal dysfunction (OIED) have been reported in individuals taking opioids. The risk of OIED may increase as the dose and/or duration of opioids increases. Regularly evaluate individuals for signs and symptoms of OIED (e.g., dysphagia, regurgitation, non-cardiac chest pain), and, if necessary, adjust therapy as clinically appropriate.

    Headline
    elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic encephalopathy, hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, hepatitis, hyperbilirubinemia, jaundice
    General Information
    Although not specifically noted during combination buprenorphine; naloxone therapy, increased intracholedochal pressure (incidence unstated), and hepatic reactions have been reported during use of buprenorphine. Baseline and periodic liver function tests are recommended during therapy. Cases of elevated hepatic enzymes, cytolytic hepatitis, hepatitis with jaundice (with hyperbilirubinemia), hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, hepatorenal syndrome, and hepatic encephalopathy have been reported. Use this medication with caution in patients with pre-existing biliary tract disorders. Pre-existing hepatic abnormalities, infection with hepatitis B or C, concomittant use of other hepatotoxic medications, and continued use of injectable drugs has been noted in many cases. The causality or contributory nature of buprenorphine to these events has not been definitively established; however, discontinuation of buprenorphine has ameliorated the hepatic reaction in some cases. Consider carefully discontinuing buprenorphine; naloxone in patients who develop hepatotoxicity.

    Headline
    anxiety, blurred vision, depression, dizziness, drowsiness, impaired cognition, psychomotor impairment
    General Information
    Buprenorphine; naloxone may depress CNS function resulting in impaired cognition and psychomotor impairment. Use may affect ability to drive or perform other hazardous tasks. When given concurrently with other CNS depressant drugs such as other narcotics, general anesthetics, benzodiazepines, phenothiazines, sedative hypnotics, alcohol, or tranquilizers the CNS depressive effects may worsen. Consider a reduction in the dose of one or both agents in situations of concomitant use. Further advise patients to avoid self-administration of other CNS depressants during therapy. Anxiety (11—14%), depression (9—13%), dizziness (2—6%), nervousness (5—7%), and somnolence or drowsiness (3—7%) have been reported during clinical study of single ingredient buprenorphine; although not specifically noted with buprenorphine; naloxone, similar incidences of these events would be expected. Intoxication, disturbance in attention, and blurred vision have been reported in 1%, but less than 5% of patients receiving buprenorphine; naloxone buccal film.

    Headline
    orthostatic hypotension, palpitations, peripheral edema
    General Information
    Adverse cardiovascular effects have occurred in patient receiving buprenorphine; naloxone therapy, and may be related to peripheral vasodilation. As with other opioids, buprenorphine may infrequently cause orthostatic hypotension in ambulatory patients (precise incidence unknown). Palpitations were noted in 1% or less of patients receiving buprenorphine; naloxone sublingual film. The most frequently reported post-marketing side effect with buprenorphine; naloxone that was not experienced during clinical trials has been peripheral edema.

    Headline
    dental caries, dental pain, glossitis, hypoesthesia, oral ulceration, stomatitis, xerostomia
    General Information
    Adverse oral effects have been reported in patients receiving transmucosal forms of buprenorphine; naloxone. Oral hypoesthesia (greater than 1%), glossodynia or burning tongue (1% or less), and oral mucosal erythema (1% or less for sublingual tablets, 1% up to 5% for buccal film) were reported during clinical trials. Adverse oral side effects that have been reported during postmarketing use include glossodynia, glossitis, oral mucosal erythema, oral hypoesthesia, toothache (dental pain), and stomatitis or oral ulceration. Dry mouth (xerostomia) has been reported with use of buprenorphine single ingredient products and might occur as well. Cases of dental caries, some severe (i.e., tooth fracture, tooth loss), have been reported following the use of transmucosal buprenorphine-containing products. Reported events include cavities, tooth decay, dental abscesses/infection, rampant dental caries, tooth erosion, fillings falling out, and, in some cases, total tooth loss. Treatment for these events included tooth extraction, root canal, dental surgery, as well as other restorative procedures (i.e., fillings, crowns, implants, dentures). Multiple cases were reported in individuals without any prior history of dental problems. Refer all patients to dental care services and encourage them to receive consistent dental care. The benefits of buprenorphine; naloxone treatment clearly outweigh the risks of dental problems in people with opioid use disorder. The risk of dental problems may be reduced by the patient gently swishing and swallowing water immediately after the medicine is completely dissolved. Patients should wait 1 hour before brushing teeth following medication administration to avoid tooth damage. Dentists should perform a baseline dental evaluation and caries risk assessment, establish a dental caries preventive plan, and encourage regular dental checkups in transmucosal buprenorphine; naloxone recipients.

    Headline
    apnea, dyspnea, hypoxia, respiratory arrest, respiratory depression
    General Information
    Although the majority of cases are associated with intravenous use, respiratory depression may also occur after administration of sublingual buprenorphine; naloxone. The respiratory depressant effects of buprenorphine are increased when used in combination with other depressants (alcohol, benzodiazepines, and opioids). A number of deaths have been reported following parenteral misuse of buprenorphine, usually with benzodiazepines; concomitant benzodiazepine use appears to alter the ceiling effect on buprenorphine-induced respiratory depression. Buprenorphine can cause sleep-related breathing disorders including central sleep apnea (CSA) and sleep-related hypoxemia (hypoxia). Patients with preexisting lung disease may be at increased risk of developing respiratory depression. In the event of an overdose, naloxone may not be effective in fully reversing the respiratory depression produced by buprenorphine; higher than normal doses of naloxone and repeated administration may be necessary. If indicated, administer oxygen, intravenous fluids, vasopressors, and other supportive measures. Slow or trouble breathing (dyspnea), apnea, and respiratory arrest may occur in overdose. Closely monitor the patient's respiratory and cardiac status and establish a patent airway with institution of mechanical ventilation if needed.

    Headline
    serotonin syndrome
    General Information
    Serotonin syndrome has been reported in patients taking opioids at recommended doses. Patients taking opioids concomitantly with a serotonergic medication should seek immediate medical attention if they develop symptoms such as agitation, hallucinations, tachycardia, fever, excessive sweating, shivering or shaking, muscle twitching or stiffness, trouble with coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea. Symptoms generally present within hours to days of taking an opioid with another serotonergic agent, but may also occur later, particularly after a dosage increase. If serotonin syndrome is suspected, either the opioid and/or the other agent should be discontinued.

    Headline
    amenorrhea, gonadal suppression, hyponatremia, impotence (erectile dysfunction), infertility, libido decrease, SIADH
    General Information
    Opioid agonists can interfere with the endocrine system by inhibiting the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), cortisol, and luteinizing hormone (LH), and by stimulating secretion of prolactin, growth hormone (GH), insulin, and glucagon. Chronic opioid use may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to hormonal changes that may manifest as hypogonadism (gonadal suppression). Although the exact causal role of opioids in the clinical manifestations of hypogonadism is unknown, patients could experience libido decrease, impotence (erectile dysfunction), amenorrhea, or infertility. Other various medical, physical, lifestyle, and psychological stressors may influence gonadal hormone concentrations; these stressors have not been adequately controlled for in clinical studies with opioids. Patients presenting with signs or symptoms of androgen deficiency should undergo laboratory evaluation. Opioid agonists can inhibit the release of thyrotropin, leading to a decrease in thyroid hormone. Morphine and related compounds can stimulate the release of vasopressin (ADH). Hyponatremia can occur as a result of SIADH.

    Headline
    adrenocortical insufficiency
    General Information
    Monitor treated patients for symptoms of opioid-induced endocrinopathy, particularly those receiving a daily dose equivalent to 100 mg or more of morphine. Opioids inhibit the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), cortisol, and luteinizing hormone (LH). In general opioids do not appear to alter thyroid function in any meaningful way, but thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) may be stimulated by opioids. Rarely, adrenocortical insufficiency has been reported in association with opioid use. Advise patients to seek immediate medical attention if they experience symptoms such as nausea, vomiting, loss of appetite, fatigue, weakness, dizziness, or hypotension. If adrenocortical insufficiency is suspected, confirm with diagnostic testing as soon as possible. If diagnosed, treat the individual with physiologic replacement doses of corticosteroids, and if appropriate, wean off of opioid therapy. If the opioid can be discontinued, a follow-up assessment of adrenal function should be performed to determine if corticosteroid treatment can be discontinued. Other opioids may be tried as some cases reported use of a different opioid without recurrence of adrenal insufficiency. The information available does not identify any particular opioids as being more likely to be associated with adrenal insufficiency.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      adrenocortical insufficiency
      Severity
      Moderate
    Headline
    QT prolongation
    General Information
    Thorough QT studies with buprenorphine products have demonstrated QT prolongation 15 msec or less. This QTc prolongation effect does not appear to be mediated by hERG channels. Based on these findings, buprenorphine is unlikely to be pro-arrhythmic when used alone in patients without risk factors. The risk of combining buprenorphine with other QT-prolonging agents or in patients at risk for QT prolongation is not known. In a clinical trial of patients receiving buprenorphine, levomethadyl, or methadone for treatment of opioid dependence, buprenorphine use was associated with less effect on QT interval than either levomethadyl or methadone use; an absolute QTc value greater than 500 milliseconds occurred in 0 of 54 patients receiving buprenorphine, 6 of 52 patients receiving methadone, and 5 of 46 patients receiving levomethadyl during the 17-week trial. The odds ratio of a QTc change of more than 60 milliseconds, considered a clinically significant change, was calculated as 15.8 with the use of levomethadyl vs. buprenorphine (95% CI: 3.7 to 67.1) and as 8.4 with the use of methadone vs. buprenorphine (95% CI: 1.9 to 36.4). According to the investigators, study patients receiving buprenorphine rarely experienced a QTc change greater than 60 milliseconds.

    Headline
    neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome, neonatal respiratory depression
    General Information
    Neonatal seizures and 1 case of neonatal respiratory depression with apnea and bradycardia have been reported in neonates exposed to buprenorphine; naloxone in utero. Neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome (NOWS) may occur in newborns exposed to buprenorphine; naloxone in utero. Data from postmarketing reports indicate the time to onset ranges from day 1 to day 8 of life, with the majority of neonates experiencing NOWS on day 1. Severe symptoms may require pharmacologic therapy managed by clinicians familiar with neonatal opioid withdrawal. Monitor the neonate for withdrawal symptoms including rapid breathing, irritability, hyperactivity, abnormal sleep pattern, high-pitched crying, tremors, vomiting, diarrhea, and failure to gain weight. Onset, duration, and severity of opioid withdrawal may vary based on the specific opioid used, duration of use, timing and amount of last maternal use, and rate of drug elimination by the newborn.

Description
  Text
  Buprenorphine; naloxone is a combination of medications administered for the treatment of opiate agonist dependence; a variety of sublingual and buccal products are available. Buprenorphine is a semisynthetic mixed opiate agonist-antagonist and naloxone is an opiate antagonist. Buprenorphine exhibits a ceiling to its pharmacological effects; thus, the danger of overdose, abuse liability, and toxicity from buprenorphine may be less than with full opioid agonists. The low-dose naloxone component mitigates abuse potential by injection. As a result, buprenorphine; naloxone is advantageous for maintenance therapy in the setting of unsupervised opiate dependence therapy. Use of buprenorphine; naloxone in-office treatment of opioid dependence was initially restricted to providers with a unique identification number (known as an X-waiver) under the U.S. Federal Drug Addiction Treatment Act (DATA). However, these restrictions were lifted in 2023 with the passage of the Consolidated Appropriations act, allowing all practitioners who are eligible to prescribe DEA schedule III medications with a current DEA registration to prescribe buprenorphine; naloxone for the treatment of opiate dependence. Induction therapy with buprenorphine; naloxone is only indicated in patients with dependence on heroin or other short-acting opioids. Buprenorphine monotherapy is recommended for induction therapy in patients with dependence on long-acting opioids, since these patients may be more susceptible to precipitated and prolonged withdrawal during induction and naloxone in the combination products is absorbed in small amounts and could worsen withdrawal. Following induction, patients with dependence on long-acting opioids may be transitioned to buprenorphine; naloxone. Methadone and buprenorphine produce similar efficacy at improving outcomes during maintenance therapy; buprenorphine is typically associated with less sedation and respiratory depression during induction therapy. Some literature suggests that methadone maintenance programs might be associated with higher rates of patient retention.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  This monograph discusses combination products of buprenorphine; naloxone when used for the treatment of opioid dependence.
 
Buprenorphine: Buprenorphine is a partial agonist at the mu-opioid receptor and an antagonist at the kappa-opioid receptor. Opiate agonists are believed to exert their effects by stimulating specific opiate receptors, designated as mu, kappa, and delta, which have been reclassified by an International Union of Pharmacology subcommittee as OP1 (delta), OP2 (kappa), and OP3 (mu). Mu-receptors are considered the classic morphine-receptor type, as stimulation at this receptor produces supraspinal analgesia, respiratory depression, euphoria, and physical dependence. Actions at kappa-receptors are believed to produce alterations in the perception of pain and the emotional response to pain. Dysphoria is often experienced due to kappa-receptor activation. It is hypothesized that patients with opioid or cocaine drug dependence have increased kappa-receptor activity to modulate the mu-receptor agonistic effects. Buprenorphine increases mu-receptor activity and blocks kappa-receptor activity. Thus, patients with opioid dependence are more likely to experience euthymia with buprenorphine versus patients that receive naloxone or naltrexone, which block mu-receptors leaving the effects of kappa-receptor agonism unopposed.Buprenorphine binds mu-receptors with high affinity; however, buprenorphine has less intrinsic activity at mu-receptors than full agonists. Blockade of the effects of full mu-receptor agonists occurs after acute administration of buprenorphine. Buprenorphine very slowly dissociates from the mu-receptor, which likely accounts for its longer duration of action than morphine, the unpredictability of its reversal by opioid antagonists, and its low level of manifested physical dependence. The pharmacologic effects observed after opiates bind to their receptors involve a second messenger such as cyclic AMP, which is synthesized by adenylate cyclase. Opioid receptors are coupled to these second messenger systems through an inhibitory G-protein (guanine nucleotide-binding protein). G-proteins are located at the cell surface along with many other receptors, including opioid receptors. G-proteins are thought to interact with opiate receptors, giving the receptor a higher affinity for the opiate. Binding of the opiate stimulates the exchange of guanosine triphosphate (GTP) for guanosine diphosphate (GDP) on the G-protein complex. Binding of GTP leads to a release of the G-protein subunit, which acts on the effector system. In this case, the effector system is adenylate cyclase and cyclic AMP located at the inner surface of the plasma membrane. Opioid agonists effectively inhibit adenylate cyclase and cause a decrease in intracellular cyclic AMP levels. Other research has shown that mu-, delta-, and kappa-receptors are associated with ion channels and control the influx of cations into the cell. Mu- and delta-receptor stimulation is associated with increasing potassium influx and kappa-receptor activity is associated with reducing calcium influx in cells located in various human and animal nerve systems. All of these effects appear to ultimately reduce transmitter release and may also be mediated through G-proteins.
Naloxone: Naloxone is an antagonist at the mu-receptor; in this combination product, it is added to buprenorphine to deter intravenous administration of the tablet formulation. When this combination is administered sublingually or buccally, any effect of naloxone is minimal due to very low absorption. Data from opioid-dependent patients indicate a parenteral to sublingual potency ratio up to 20:1. The presence of naloxone does not affect the efficacy of buprenorphine when the combination is given sublingually or buccally; however, if the combination is given intravenously, buprenorphine-induced mu-receptor agonism is neutralized.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Sublingual Administration (tablets and sublingual film):
Buprenorphine displays poor oral bioavailability; however, bioavailability improves when buprenorphine is administered sublingually and plasma concentrations are linear across the therapeutic range.
Though not all doses and dose combinations of Suboxone sublingual tablets and film met bioequivalence criteria, the pharmacokinetics of buprenorphine; naloxone are similar between the formulations.
In several pharmacokinetic studies following the administration of different dosages, a dose of one or two of the 2 mg/0.5 mg Suboxone sublingual films administered sublingually showed comparable relative bioavailability to the same total dose of the Suboxone sublingual tablets.
One 8 mg/2 mg and one 12 mg/3 mg sublingual films showed higher relative bioavailability for both buprenorphine and naloxone compared to the same total dose of Suboxone sublingual tablets.
A combination of one 8 mg/2 mg and two 2 mg/0.5 mg Suboxone sublingual films (total dose of 12 mg/3 mg) administered sublingually showed comparable relative bioavailability to the same total dose of Suboxone sublingual tablets.
The Zubsolv sublingual film has a different bioavailability than the Suboxone sublingual tablet. One Zubsolv 5.7 mg/1.4 mg tablet provides equivalent buprenorphine exposure and 12% lower naloxone exposure to one Suboxone 8 mg/2 mg tablet.
The bioavailability of naloxone when administered sublingually is poor resulting in low plasma concentrations. For most patients, the naloxone plasma concentration after taking the Suboxone sublingual tablet at doses of 1 to 4 mg is undetectable (less than 0.05 ng/mL) beyond 2 hours.
 
Buccal Administration of Suboxone sublingual film:
Overall, the pharmacokinetic parameters and exposures resulting from the buccal and sublingual administrations of Suboxone sublingual film are comparable; however, buccal administration is not recommended during induction treatment since naloxone exposure is higher with the buccal route than the sublingual route and there is a risk of precipitating withdrawal. Buprenorphine exposure is similar between the buccal and sublingual route; therefore, patients can switch between buccal and sublingual administration without significant risk of under or overdosing during maintenance treatment.
A dose of one or two of the 2 mg/0.5 mg Suboxone sublingual films administered buccally showed comparable relative bioavailability to the same total dose of Suboxone sublingual tablets.
One dose of 8 mg/2 mg and one dose of 12 mg/3 mg Suboxone sublingual films administered buccally showed higher relative bioavailability for both buprenorphine and naloxone versus the same total dose of Suboxone sublingual tablets.
A combination of one 8 mg/2 mg and two 2 mg/0.5 mg Suboxone sublingual films administered buccally showed higher relative bioavailability compared to the same total dose of Suboxone sublingual tablets.
 
Buccal Administration of Bunavail buccal film:
Plasma levels of buprenorphine and naloxone increased with the buccal dose of buprenorphine; naloxone (Bunavail) buccal film. There was wide inter-patient variability in the buccal absorption of buprenorphine and naloxone, but intra-patient variability was low.
The bioavailability of the buccal film has been shown to be different compared to Suboxone sublingual tablet or equivalent generics; thus, there is different dosing for the buccal film relative to the sublingual tablets. The exposure of buprenorphine from one buprenorphine; naloxone 4.2 mg/0.7 mg buccal film was equivalent to one Suboxone 8 mg/2 mg sublingual tablet; however, the naloxone exposure from the buccal film was 33% less than with Suboxone sublingual tablets.
As the dose of buprenorphine increased (dose range: 0.875 mg to 6.3 mg), both the Cmax and AUC increased, although the increase was not directly dose-proportional. Naloxone did not affect the pharmacokinetics of buprenorphine.
The coadministration of liquids with the buccal film reduced the systemic exposure up to 59% and 76% for buprenorphine and naloxone, respectively.
    Name
    Intravenous Route
    Description
    Buprenorphine; naloxone products are not approved or recommended for intravenous administration. An opioid withdrawal syndrome is likely to occur with parenteral misuse of buprenorphine; naloxone by individuals physically dependent on full opioid agonists.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Avoid use of buprenorphine; naloxone in patients with severe hepatic impairment. Buprenorphine and naloxone are extensively metabolized in the liver. In patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment, plasma levels of both agents are higher and the half-lives are longer. The effect of hepatic impairment on naloxone is much greater than buprenorphine, and the subsequent clinical impact is likely to be greater as the severity of hepatic impairment increases. Use may not be appropriate in patients with moderate hepatic impairment because elevated levels of naloxone may precipitate opiate withdrawal; the product should be used cautiously if treatment is necessary and with close monitoring since elevated naloxone levels may reduce the efficacy of buprenorphine throughout treatment. No clinically significant changes have been observed in those with mild hepatic impairment and no dosage adjustments are required.
 
In subjects with hepatitis C virus (HCV) infection but no sign of hepatic impairment, the changes in the mean Cmax, AUC, and half-life values of buprenorphine and naloxone were not clinically significant in comparison to healthy subjects without HCV infection.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    No differences in buprenorphine pharmacokinetics were observed between 9 dialysis-dependent and 6 normal patients following IV administration of 0.3 mg buprenorphine. The effects of renal failure on naloxone pharmacokinetics are unknown.
  Text
  Buprenorphine; naloxone is administered orally via the sublingual and buccal route. Buprenorphine is approximately 96% protein bound, primarily to alpha and beta globulin. Naloxone is approximately 45% protein bound, primarily to albumin. The addition of naloxone to buprenorphine does not affect the pharmacokinetics of buprenorphine. Buprenorphine is partially metabolized by N-dealkylation through CYP3A4 to an active metabolite, norbuprenorphine. Both buprenorphine and norbuprenorphine undergo glucuronidation. Naloxone undergoes extensive hepatic metabolism by direct glucuronidation, N-dealkylation, and reduction. The half-life of buprenorphine from Suboxone and Zubsolv sublingual film ranges from 24 to 42 hours and the half-life of naloxone ranges from 2 to 12 hours. The half-life of buprenorphine from Bunavail buccal film ranges from 16.4 to 27.5 hours and the half-life of naloxone ranges from 1.9 to 2.4 hours. Excretion of a buprenorphine dose occurs renally (30%) and in the feces (69%) as buprenorphine, norbuprenorphine, and two unidentified metabolites. The majority of buprenorphine and norbuprenorphine eliminated in the urine is conjugated (20.4%) with only 1% of buprenorphine and 2.7% of norbuprenorphine in free form. In feces, the majority of a dose is in the form of free buprenorphine (33%) and free norbuprenorphine (21%), with a lower percentage as conjugated buprenorphine and norbuprenorphine (7%).
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP3A4
Buprenorphine is metabolized to its major metabolite, norbuprenorphine, primarily by CYP3A4. Concomitant treatment with CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., ketoconazole, protease inhibitors, macrolide antibiotics) or CYP3A4 inducers (e.g., phenytoin, carbamazepine, rifampin) may alter buprenorphine plasma concentrations, resulting in toxicity or decreased efficacy, respectively. Buprenorphine has been shown to be a CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 inhibitor and in vitro studies have shown that norbuprenorphine is a modest CYP2D6 inhibitor; however, the relatively low plasma concentrations of buprenorphine and norbuprenorphine resulting from therapeutic doses are not expected to result in clinically relevant interactions.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Solid Formulations
      Description
      General information for all transmucosal dosage forms (sublingual and buccal):
The risk for dental disease may be increased. Encourage all patients to receive consistent dental care and checkups while using these products. Patients may lessen the risk of dental problems by swishing and swallowing water immediately after a dose is completely administered and dissolved. Patients should wait 1 hour before brushing teeth to reduce the risk for tooth damage.
Storage: Store buprenorphine; naloxone in a location out of the reach of children, pets, and others for whom the drug is not prescribed.
Disposal: Follow the product specific instructions for safe disposal of unused buprenorphine; naloxone.
 
Sublingual tablet administration (Suboxone generic equivalent sublingual tablets)
Place tablet under tongue to dissolve; allow the tablet to completely dissolve.
Do not swallow or chew the tablets. Instruct patients to be consistent with tablet administration; swallowing the tablet will reduce the bioavailability of the drug.
For doses requiring more than 2 tablets per dose, advise patients to either place all the tablets under the tongue at once or, if they cannot fit more than 2 tablets comfortably, place 2 tablets at a time under the tongue. With either option, patients should hold the tablets under the tongue until they completely dissolve.
 
Sublingual tablet administration (Zubsolv sublingual tablets)
Place tablet under tongue to dissolve; allow the tablet to completely dissolve.
Do not cut, chew, or swallow the tablets as this will reduce the bioavailability of the drug.
For doses requiring more than 1 tablet, advise the patient to place all tablets in different places under the tongue at the same time. If a sequential mode of administration is preferred, patients should follow the same manner of dosing with continued use of the product, to ensure consistency in bioavailability.
Advise patients not to eat or drink anything until the tablet is completely dissolved.
 
Sublingual film administration (Suboxone sublingual film)
Do not cut, chew, or swallow the sublingual film.
The package should not be opened until the patient is ready to use the medication. The foil package should be opened by folding along the dotted lines and tearing down at slits or cutting with scissors along the arrow. After opening, the film should be used right away.
Before taking the medication, drink water to moisten the inside of the mouth. This helps the film dissolve more easily.
Place the film under tongue; allow the film to completely dissolve.
Do not move the film once placed, as this will reduce the bioavailability of the drug.
For doses requiring more than 1 film per dose, advise the patient to place the films on opposite sides of the mouth under the tongue; avoid letting the films touch.
Proper administration technique should be demonstrated to the patient.
 
Buccal administration of sublingual film (Suboxone sublingual film for buccal use)
Do not cut, chew, or swallow the film.
The foil package should not be opened until the patient is ready to use the medication. The foil package should be opened by folding along the dotted lines and tearing down at slits or cutting with scissors along the arrow. After opening, the film should be used right away.
Before taking the medication, drink water to moisten the inside of the mouth. This helps the film dissolve more easily.
Place 1 film on the inside of the right or left cheek and allow to dissolve completely.
If an additional film is necessary to achieve the prescribed dose, place an additional film on the inside of the opposite cheek and allow to dissolve completely.
If a third film is necessary to achieve the prescribed dose, place the third film on the inside of the right or left cheek after the first 2 films have dissolved.
Do not move the film after placement since this will reduce the bioavailability of the drug.
Proper administration technique should be demonstrated to the patient.
 
Buccal film administration (Bunavail buccal film)
Do not open the foil package until you are ready to use the buprenorphine; naloxone buccal film. Open the foil package by folding along the dotted lines and tearing down at slits or cut with scissors in the direction of the arrows. After opening, use the film right away.
Use the tongue to wet the inside of the cheek or rinse the mouth with water to moisten the area immediately before placement of the film.
Hold the film with clean, dry fingers with the text (BN2, BN4, or BN6) facing up.
Place the side of the film with the text (BN2, BN4, or BN6) against the inside of the moistened cheek.
Press and hold the film in place for 5 seconds.
The film will stick to the inside of your cheek; leave the film in place until it completely dissolves.
If multiple films need to be administered, immediately apply the next film according to the steps above. Note that when 2 films are required for one dose, 1 film should be placed on the inside of 1 cheek and the other film on the inside of the other cheek. For doses requiring 3 films, place the third film on the inside of the right or left cheek after the first 2 films have dissolved.
Avoid touching or moving the film(s) with the tongue or finger(s).
Avoid drinking or eating food until the film(s) dissolve.
Do not chew or swallow the film, as this may result in lower peak concentrations and lower bioavailability.
Use the entire film; do not cut or tear it.
    Name
    Oral Administration

Zubsolv sublingual tablets: 17.1 mg/4.2 mg per day sublingually.
Bunavail buccal film: 12.6 mg/2.1 mg per day buccally.
 
Less than 16 years:
Safety and efficacy have not been established.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Mild hepatic impairment: No dosage adjustments are necessary.
 
Moderate hepatic impairment: Hepatic impairment results in a reduced clearance of naloxone to a much greater extent than buprenorphine. Induction treatment with these products is not recommended in patients with moderate hepatic impairment, as there is an increased risk of precipitated withdrawal at the beginning of treatment. Buprenorphine/naloxone products may be used with caution for maintenance treatment in patients with moderate hepatic impairment who have initiated treatment on a buprenorphine product without naloxone. Patients should be carefully monitored and consideration given to the possibility of naloxone interfering with buprenorphine's efficacy during maintenance treatment.
 
Severe hepatic impairment: Use should be avoided.
  Renal Impairment
  Specific guidelines for dosage adjustments in renal impairment are not available; it appears that no dosage adjustments are required.
 
Intermittent Hemodialysis
No differences in buprenorphine pharmacokinetics were observed between 9 patients receiving dialysis and 6 normal patients following IV administration of 0.3 mg buprenorphine. The effects of renal failure on naloxone kinetics are unknown.
